17:T221f,Digestion is the hydrolytic breakdown of complex, insoluble organic macromolecules into simple, diffusible, and absorbable micromolecules, mediated by mechanical mastication and hydrolytic digestive enzymes.

```
                         THE ALIMENTARY TRACT
                         
  Mouth / Buccal Cavity (Teeth, Tongue, Salivary Amylase)
          │
          ▼
  Pharynx & Esophagus (Deglutition, Peristalsis, Epiglottis)
          │
          ▼  [Gastroesophageal Sphincter]
  Stomach (Cardia, Fundus, Body, Pylorus; HCl, Pepsin, Intrinsic Factor)
          │
          ▼  [Pyloric Sphincter]
  Small Intestine: Duodenum ──► Jejunum ──► Ileum (Crypts of Lieberkuhn, Villi)
          │        ▲         ▲
          │        │ (Bile)  │ (Pancreatic Juice)
          │     [Liver]   [Pancreas]
          ▼  [Ileocecal Valve]
  Large Intestine: Cecum (Appendix) ──► Colon (Ascending, Transverse, Descending, Sigmoid) ──► Rectum
```

### 24.1.1 Dentition & Oral Digestion
* **Human Dental Architecture:**
  * **Thecodont:** Teeth are embedded in deep bony sockets of the maxilla and mandible.
  * **Diphyodont:** Two successive sets of teeth develop during lifetime: deciduous/milk teeth ($20\text{ teeth}$) replaced by permanent adult dentition ($32\text{ teeth}$).
  * **Heterodont:** Four morphologically and functionally distinct dental morphotypes:
    1. *Incisors (I):* Chisel-shaped; specialized for cutting and biting.
    2. *Canines (C):* Dagger-shaped, sharp; specialized for tearing flesh.
    3. *Premolars (PM):* Bicuspid crowns; specialized for grinding and crushing.
    4. *Molars (M):* Broad triturating surfaces; crushing and chewing.
* **Adult Human Dental Formula:**
  $$\text{Dental Formula} = \frac{I\text{ }2,\text{ }C\text{ }1,\text{ }PM\text{ }2,\text{ }M\text{ }3}{I\text{ }2,\text{ }C\text{ }1,\text{ }PM\text{ }2,\text{ }M\text{ }3} \times 2 = \frac{2123}{2123} \times 2 = \mathbf{32\text{ Permanent Teeth}}$$
  *(Deciduous Child Formula: $\frac{2102}{2102} \times 2 = 20\text{ teeth}$; premolars are completely absent).*
* **Enamel:** The outermost hard, acellular, calcified layer covering the tooth crown; derived from ectoderm (ameloblasts). Composed of **Calcium Hydroxyapatite [$\text{Ca}_{10}(\text{PO}_4)_6(\text{OH})_2$]**; the **hardest biological substance in the human body**.
* **Salivary Digestion:** Three pairs of salivary glands—**Parotid** (largest, below ear; infected by Mumps paramyxovirus), **Submandibular / Submaxillary** (at the angle of lower jaw), and **Sublingual** (below tongue)—secrete $1.0 - 1.5\text{ liters/day}$ of saliva ($\text{pH} \approx 6.8$):
  * **Ptyalin (Salivary $\alpha$-Amylase):** Hydrolyzes approximately **$30\%$ of dietary starch** into the disaccharide maltose:
    $$\text{Starch} \xrightarrow[\text{pH } 6.8, \text{ Cl}^- \text{ activator}]{\text{Salivary Amylase}} \text{Maltose} + \text{Isomaltose} + \alpha\text{-limit dextrins}$$
  * **Lysozyme:** Cleaves $\beta(1 \to 4)$ bonds in bacterial peptidoglycan walls, acting as an antimicrobial barrier.

### 24.1.2 Gastric Digestion & Mucosal Cytology
The stomach is a J-shaped muscular reservoir churning food into a semi-fluid acidic slurry called **Chyme**. The gastric mucosa features gastric pits lined by specialized secretory cells:
1. **Parietal (Oxyntic) Cells:**
   * Secrete **Hydrochloric Acid (HCl)** ($\text{pH} \approx 1.5 - 2.0$), which creates an acidic medium killing ingested pathogens, denatures dietary proteins, and activates inactive zymogens.
   * Secrete **Castle's Intrinsic Factor (CIF)**: A glycoprotein essential for the receptor-mediated endocytosis of **Vitamin $\text{B}_{12}$ (Cyanocobalamin)** in the terminal ileum. Autoimmune destruction of parietal cells causes **Pernicious Anemia**.
2. **Peptic (Chief / Zymogen) Cells:** Secrete inactive proenzymes:
   * **Pepsinogen:** Cleaved autocatalytically by $\text{HCl}$ into active **Pepsin**, an endopeptidase hydrolyzing proteins into proteoses and peptones:
     $$\text{Pepsinogen} \xrightarrow{\text{HCl / Pepsin}} \text{Pepsin}; \quad \text{Proteins} \xrightarrow{\text{Pepsin}} \text{Proteoses} + \text{Peptones}$$
   * **Prorennin:** Secreted in infant gastric juice; converted by $\text{HCl}$ to **Rennin** (chymosin), which curdles soluble milk casein into insoluble calcium paracaseinate in the presence of $\text{Ca}^{2+}$, preventing rapid gastric emptying. (Absent in adult humans).
3. **Mucous Neck Cells:** Secrete alkaline bicarbonate-rich mucus that coats gastric mucosa, shielding it against excoriation and autodigestion by concentrated $\text{HCl}$ and pepsin. Breakdown of this protective mucosal barrier by *Helicobacter pylori* or NSAIDs results in **Peptic Ulcers**.

### 24.1.3 Hepatobiliary & Pancreatic Digestion
* **The Liver (Largest Gland, $\approx 1.5\text{ kg}$):** Composed of hexagonal **Hepatic Lobules** (structural and functional units) covered by **Glisson's Capsule**. Sinusoids contain resident phagocytic macrophages called **Kupffer Cells**, which destroy senescent RBCs and bacteria.
  * *Bile Production:* Hepatocytes continuously synthesize **Bile Juice** ($\text{pH} \approx 7.8 - 8.6$), concentrated and stored in the **Gallbladder**.
  * **Bile Juice Contains ZERO Digestive Enzymes!**
  * *Bile Salts:* **Sodium Glycocholate and Sodium Taurocholate** reduce surface tension, breaking massive lipid globules into microscopic droplets (**Emulsification**), vastly expanding the surface area for lipase activity. Bile salts also form water-soluble **Micelles** required for lipid absorption.
  * *Bile Pigments:* **Bilirubin** (golden-yellow) and **Biliverdin** (green), catabolic degradation products of hemoglobin heme. Hyperbilirubinemia ($>2\text{ mg/dL}$) causes systemic yellow discoloration of sclera and skin (**Jaundice / Icterus**).
* **The Pancreas:** A heterocrine (mixed) gland:
  * *Exocrine Pancreatic Juice ($\text{pH} \approx 8.4$):* Discharged into the duodenum via the Hepatopancreatic Ampulla of Vater, guarded by the **Sphincter of Oddi**:
    1. **Trypsinogen:** Activated to **Trypsin** by intestinal brush-border enzyme **Enterokinase (Enteropeptidase)**. Trypsin then autocatalytically activates all other pancreatic zymogens:
       $$\text{Trypsinogen} \xrightarrow{\text{Enterokinase}} \text{Trypsin}$$
       $$\text{Chymotrypsinogen} \xrightarrow{\text{Trypsin}} \text{Chymotrypsin}; \quad \text{Procarboxypeptidase} \xrightarrow{\text{Trypsin}} \text{Carboxypeptidase}$$
    2. **Pancreatic Amylase (Amylopsin):** Hydrolyzes remaining starches into disaccharides.
    3. **Pancreatic Lipase (Steapsin):** The primary enzyme hydrolyzing emulsified triglycerides into monoglycerides and free fatty acids.
    4. **Nucleases:** Deoxyribonuclease (DNase) and Ribonuclease (RNase) hydrolyze nucleic acids into nucleotides.

### 24.1.4 Intestinal Digestion & Absorption Kinetics
The small intestine is the definitive organ of complete enzymatic digestion and nutrient absorption:
* **Succus Entericus (Intestinal Juice):** Secreted by the **Crypts of Lieberkühn**: contains disaccharidases (**Maltase, Lactase, Sucrase**), aminopeptidases, dipeptidases, nucleosidases, and intestinal lipases.
* **Surface Area Amplification:** The absorptive surface of the small intestine is amplified $>600\text{-fold}$ through three architectural levels:
  1. *Plicae Circulares (Valves of Kerckring):* Macroscopic permanent circular mucosal folds ($3\times$ amplification).
  2. *Villi:* Millions of finger-like projections ($10\times$ amplification).
  3. *Microvilli:* Apical brush border displaying $\sim 200\text{ million microvilli/mm}^2$ ($20\times$ amplification), yielding a net surface area exceeding $250\text{ m}^2$.
* **Nutrient Absorption Pathways:**
  * *Glucose and Galactose:* Absorbed via **Secondary Active Transport** coupled to $Na^+$ influx via the **SGLT-1** symporter on the apical membrane, followed by facilitated diffusion via **GLUT-2** into mesenteric capillaries.
  * *Fructose:* Absorbed exclusively by **Facilitated Diffusion** via **GLUT-5** (no energy required).
  * *Amino Acids:* Absorbed predominantly via $Na^+$-dependent active transport symporters.
  * *Lipids:* Free fatty acids and 2-monoglycerides diffuse passively from micelles into mucosal enterocytes, where they are re-esterified into triglycerides within the SER, packaged with apolipoproteins and cholesterol into spherical water-soluble droplets called **Chylomicrons** ($\approx 1000\text{ \AA}$ diameter). Chylomicrons are discharged via exocytosis into central lymphatic capillaries called **Lacteals**, bypassing the hepatic portal circulation to enter the bloodstream via the thoracic duct.

---18:T2d02,Respiration involves external ventilation, alveolar gas diffusion, cardiovascular transport, systemic tissue exchange, and cellular oxidative phosphorylation.

```
                      THE RESPIRATORY PASSAGEWAY
                      
  External Nostrils ──► Nasal Chamber ──► Pharynx (Nasopharynx) ──► Glottis (Epiglottis)
          │
          ▼
  Larynx ("Voice Box", Thyroid / Cricoid Cartilages, Vocal Cords)
          │
          ▼
  Trachea (Incomplete C-shaped Hyaline Cartilaginous Rings; bifurcates at T5 vertebra)
          │
          ▼
  Right & Left Primary Bronchi ──► Secondary / Tertiary Bronchi ──► Terminal Bronchioles
          │
          ▼
  Respiratory Bronchioles ──► Alveolar Ducts ──► ALVEOLAR SACS (Terminal Respiratory Unit)
```

### 24.2.1 Biomechanics of Pulmonary Ventilation
Lungs are situated in the airtight thoracic cavity enclosed laterally by ribs and intercostal muscles, dorsally by the vertebral column, ventrally by the sternum, and inferiorly by the dome-shaped muscular **Diaphragm**.
* **Inspiration (Active Process):**
  1. Contraction of the **Diaphragm** causes it to flatten inferiorly, increasing the antero-posterior thoracic volume.
  2. Contraction of the **External Intercostal Muscles** lifts the ribs and sternum upward and outward, expanding the dorso-ventral thoracic diameter.
  3. Increased thoracic volume decreases intra-pulmonary pressure below atmospheric pressure ($\approx -1\text{ to }-3\text{ mmHg}$ relative to atmosphere), sucking air into the lungs.
* **Expiration (Passive Process at Rest):**
  1. Relaxation of the diaphragm (resuming its convex dome shape) and external intercostals reduces thoracic volume.
  2. Elastic recoil of the pulmonary parenchyma elevates intra-pulmonary pressure above atmospheric pressure ($+1\text{ to }+3\text{ mmHg}$), expelling air.
  3. *Forced Expiration:* Active process recruiting **Internal Intercostal Muscles** and abdominal wall muscles (rectus abdominis).
* **Pulmonary Surfactant:** Synthesized by **Type II Alveolar Pneumocytes**; composed predominantly of **Dipalmitoylphosphatidylcholine (DPPC)**. Dramatically reduces surface tension at the alveolar air-liquid interface, preventing alveolar collapse (atelectasis) during expiration. Deficiency in premature neonates causes fatal **Infant Respiratory Distress Syndrome (IRDS)**.

### 24.2.2 Pulmonary Volumes and Capacities (Spirometry)

```
       Volume (mL)
          ▲
     6000 ┼ ------------------------------------------------------------- Total Lung Capacity (5800 mL)
          │                                 /\
          │                                /  \    <-- Inspiratory Reserve Volume (IRV: ~3000 mL)
     3000 ┼ ------------------------------/----\------------------------
          │                   /\         /      \  <-- Tidal Volume (TV: 500 mL)
     2500 ┼ -----------------/--\-------/--------\-----------------------
          │                 /    \     /          \ <-- Expiratory Reserve Volume (ERV: ~1100 mL)
     1200 ┼ ----------------/------\---/------------\-------------------- Vital Capacity (~4600 mL)
          │                /        \ /              \
          │  ///////////////////////////////////////// <-- Residual Volume (RV: 1200 mL, NEVER exhaled)
        0 ┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
```

| Spirometric Metric | Mathematical Definition & Normal Adult Value | Physiological & Clinical Significance |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tidal Volume (TV)** | Volume inspired or expired during a normal resting breath: **$\approx 500\text{ mL}$** (Healthy adult: $\approx 6000 - 8000\text{ mL/min}$). | Includes **$\approx 150\text{ mL}$ Anatomical Dead Space** (air filling conducting airways that does not participate in gas exchange). |
| **Inspiratory Reserve Volume (IRV)** | Extra volume inhaled forcefully beyond normal tidal volume: **$\approx 2500 - 3000\text{ mL}$**. | Reflects maximal inspiratory muscle effort and pulmonary compliance. |
| **Expiratory Reserve Volume (ERV)** | Extra volume exhaled forcefully beyond normal tidal expiration: **$\approx 1000 - 1100\text{ mL}$**. | Diminished in restrictive pulmonary disorders and obesity. |
| **Residual Volume (RV)** | Volume of gas remaining in lungs even after maximal, forced expiration: **$\approx 1100 - 1200\text{ mL}$**. | **CANNOT be measured by standard spirometry!** (requires helium dilution or plethysmography); prevents alveolar collapse. |
| **Vital Capacity (VC)** | Maximum volume exhaled after deepest possible inspiration: $\mathbf{\text{VC} = \text{TV} + \text{IRV} + \text{ERV}} \approx \mathbf{4600\text{ mL}}$. | Paramount clinical index of functional respiratory capacity. |
| **Functional Residual Capacity (FRC)** | Volume remaining in lungs after normal passive expiration: $\mathbf{\text{FRC} = \text{ERV} + \text{RV}} \approx \mathbf{2200 - 2300\text{ mL}}$. | Acts as a constant oxygen reservoir buffering blood gas fluctuations between breaths. |
| **Total Lung Capacity (TLC)** | Total gas volume contained in lungs after maximal forced inspiration: $\mathbf{\text{TLC} = \text{VC} + \text{RV}} \approx \mathbf{5800 - 6000\text{ mL}}$. | Expanded in emphysema; constricted in pulmonary fibrosis. |

### 24.2.3 Alveolar Gas Exchange & Diffusion Barrier
Gas exchange across the alveolar-capillary membrane operates strictly via **Simple Passive Diffusion** driven by partial pressure gradients:

$$\text{Diffusion Rate } \propto \frac{\Delta P \cdot A \cdot S}{d \cdot \sqrt{\text{MW}}}$$

* **Respiratory Membrane Layers (Total Thickness $< 0.5\text{ }\mu\text{m}$):**
  1. Squamous epithelium of the alveolar wall.
  2. Non-cellular basement membrane.
  3. Capillary endothelial basement membrane.
  4. Endothelial cell layer of the pulmonary blood capillary.
* **Partial Pressure Gradients:**
  * Alveolar Gas: $pO_2 = 104\text{ mmHg}, \quad pCO_2 = 40\text{ mmHg}$.
  * Deoxygenated Venous Blood: $pO_2 = 40\text{ mmHg}, \quad pCO_2 = 45\text{ mmHg}$.
  * Oxygenated Arterial Blood: $pO_2 = 95\text{ mmHg}, \quad pCO_2 = 40\text{ mmHg}$.
  * Systemic Tissues: $pO_2 \le 40\text{ mmHg}, \quad pCO_2 \ge 45\text{ mmHg}$.
* **Solubility Differential:** The solubility coefficient of **$\text{CO}_2$ is $20–25\text{ times}$ higher** than that of $\text{O}_2$. Consequently, despite a minute partial pressure gradient for $\text{CO}_2$ ($\Delta P = 5\text{ mmHg}$) compared to $\text{O}_2$ ($\Delta P = 64\text{ mmHg}$), equivalent molar quantities of $\text{CO}_2$ diffuse across the respiratory membrane per unit time.

### 24.2.4 Gas Transport Biophysics in Blood
#### 1. Oxygen ($O_2$) Transport
* **Dissolved in Plasma:** Only $\approx 1.5 - 3\%$ of total $O_2$ is transported physically dissolved in plasma water due to low solubility.
* **Bound to Hemoglobin:** $\approx 97 - 98.5\%$ is transported as **Oxyhemoglobin ($\text{Hb}_4\text{O}_8$)**. Each of the four heme $\text{Fe}^{2+}$ iron moieties in adult hemoglobin binds one $O_2$ reversibly:
  $$\text{Hb}_4 + 4\text{O}_2 \rightleftharpoons \text{Hb}_4(\text{O}_2)_4$$
  * *Oxygen Carrying Capacity:* $1\text{ gram of pure Hb}$ binds **$1.34\text{ mL of } O_2$**. With normal hemoglobin levels ($15\text{ g/dL}$), $100\text{ mL of oxygenated arterial blood}$ carries $\approx \mathbf{20\text{ mL of } O_2}$. Under resting conditions, tissues extract $\approx 5\text{ mL of } O_2$ per $100\text{ mL}$ of blood ($25\%$ delivery).

```
       % Hb Saturation
          ▲
      100 ┼                                . - - ' ' 
          │                            . '
          │                         . '      <-- LEFT SHIFT: High Affinity (Alveoli: Low Temp, Low CO2, High pH)
       50 ┼ - - - - - - - - - - . '          
          │                   . '            <-- RIGHT SHIFT (BOHR EFFECT): Low Affinity (Tissues: High Temp, High CO2, Low pH, 2,3-BPG)
          │               . '   │
          │           . '       │
          └─────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────────► pO2 (mmHg)
                   P50         P50
```

* **The Oxygen-Hemoglobin Dissociation Curve (Sigmoid Shape):** The sigmoidal contour reflects **Positive Cooperativity**—binding of the first $O_2$ molecule induces an allosteric conformational shift from the Tense ($T$) to the Relaxed ($R$) state, progressively augmenting the affinity of the remaining heme sites.
* **$P_{50}$ Value:** The partial pressure of oxygen at which hemoglobin is $50\%$ saturated with oxygen (normally $\approx 26.6\text{ mmHg}$).
* **The Bohr Effect (Right Shift $\implies$ Decreased Affinity $\implies O_2$ Unloading in Tissues):**
  * Shift to the **RIGHT** occurs when $P_{50}$ increases, favoring oxygen delivery to active tissues. Induced by:
    $$\uparrow p\text{CO}_2, \quad \uparrow [\text{H}^+] \text{ (decreased pH / Acidosis)}, \quad \uparrow \text{Temperature}, \quad \uparrow \text{2,3-Bisphosphoglycerate (2,3-BPG)}.$$
  * Shift to the **LEFT** occurs in pulmonary alveoli (decreased $p\text{CO}_2$, elevated pH, cooler temperature), maximizing oxygen loading.
  * *Fetal Hemoglobin ($\text{HbF}, \alpha_2\gamma_2$):* Lacks binding sites for 2,3-BPG, giving it a much **higher $O_2$ affinity than adult HbA**, ensuring placental oxygen transfer from maternal blood to fetal blood (curve shifted permanently to the left; $P_{50} \approx 19\text{ mmHg}$).

#### 2. Carbon Dioxide ($CO_2$) Transport
Transported in blood via three physiological mechanisms:
1. **Dissolved in Physical Solution:** $\approx 7\%$ dissolved in plasma water.
2. **Carbamino-Hemoglobin ($\text{HbCO}_2$):** $\approx 20 - 23\%$ binds reversibly to uncharged terminal amino groups ($-\text{NH}_2$) of globin polypeptide chains:
   $$\text{Hb-NH}_2 + \text{CO}_2 \rightleftharpoons \text{Hb-NH-COO}^- + \text{H}^+$$
   *(Carbon Monoxide Poisoning Distinction: Carbon monoxide binds the $\text{Fe}^{2+}$ heme ring with $>200\times$ higher affinity than $O_2$, forming **Carboxyhemoglobin**, starving tissues of oxygen).*
3. **Bicarbonate Ions ($\text{HCO}_3^-$):** $\approx \mathbf{70\%}$ (the dominant mechanism).
   * Inside erythrocytes, the zinc-metalloenzyme **Carbonic Anhydrase** accelerates the reversible hydration of $\text{CO}_2$ by a factor of $10^6$:
     $$\text{CO}_2 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \underset{\text{Anhydrase}}{\overset{\text{Carbonic}}{\rightleftharpoons}} \text{H}_2\text{CO}_3 \rightleftharpoons \text{HCO}_3^- + \text{H}^+$$
   * Released protons ($\text{H}^+$) are buffered by deoxygenated hemoglobin.
   * **The Chloride Shift (Hamburger's Phenomenon):** As $\text{HCO}_3^-$ accumulates inside erythrocytes at the tissue level, it diffuses down its concentration gradient out into the plasma via the **Anion Exchanger 1 (AE1 / Band 3)** protein. To maintain electrical neutrality, **Chloride ions ($\text{Cl}^-$) diffuse from plasma into the erythrocyte**. In pulmonary capillaries, the entire cascade reverses completely.
* **The Haldane Effect:** Deoxygenation of hemoglobin increases its capacity to carry $\text{CO}_2$ and protons, facilitating $\text{CO}_2$ uptake in tissues; conversely, oxygenation of hemoglobin in the lungs promotes the prompt displacement and release of $\text{CO}_2$.

---19:T2778,The cardiovascular system is a closed, pressurized, rapid hydraulic circuit circulating oxygen, nutrients, hormones, and metabolic wastes.

```
                         THE DUAL CIRCULATORY CIRCUIT
                         
          ┌──────────────── Pulmonary Circulation ──────────────┐
          │                                                     │
          ▼                                                     │
   [Pulmonary Capillaries] ◄─── [Pulmonary Artery] ◄───┐       │
          │                                            │       │
          ▼ (Oxygenated Blood)                         │       │
   [Pulmonary Veins]                                   │       │
          │                                            │       │
          ▼                                            │       │
   [Left Atrium] ──► [Left Ventricle]                  │       │
                            │                          │       │
                            ▼                          │       │
                         [AORTA]                       │       │
                            │                          │       │
                            ▼                          │       │
                 [Systemic Capillaries]                │       │
                            │                          │       │
                            ▼ (Deoxygenated Blood)     │       │
                     [Vena Cavae]                      │       │

                     [Right Atrium] ──► [Right Ventricle] ─────┘
                            │
                            └────────── Systemic Circulation ──────────┘
```

### 24.3.1 Blood Composition & Hemostasis
Whole blood represents $\approx 7 - 8\%$ of total body weight ($\sim 5\text{ liters}$ in adults; $\text{pH} \approx 7.35 - 7.45$).
* **Plasma ($55\%$ by volume):** Pale yellow liquid containing $90–92\%$ water and $6–8\%$ proteins:
  1. *Serum Albumin ($4.5\text{ g/dL}$):* Primary determinant of **Colloid Osmotic (Oncotic) Pressure** ($\approx 25\text{ mmHg}$), retaining intravascular fluid volume; hypoproteinemia results in generalized tissue **Edema**.
  2. *Globulins ($2.5\text{ g/dL}$):* $\alpha$- and $\beta$-globulins (transport proteins); **$\gamma$-Globulins (Immunoglobulins)** synthesized by plasma B-cells acting as humoral antibodies.
  3. *Fibrinogen ($0.3\text{ g/dL}$):* Inactive soluble clotting precursor synthesized by the liver.
  * **Serum:** Defibrinated plasma: $\mathbf{\text{Serum} = \text{Plasma} - \text{Clotting Factors}}$.
* **Formed Cellular Elements ($45\%$ by volume):**
  1. **Erythrocytes (RBCs):** Non-nucleated, biconcave circular discs ($7.2\text{ }\mu\text{m}$ diameter); average lifespan **$120\text{ days}$**. Count: $5.0 - 5.5\text{ million/mm}^3$ (males), $4.5 - 5.0\text{ million/mm}^3$ (females). Destroyed in the spleen ("graveyard of RBCs").
  2. **Leukocytes (WBCs):** Nucleated cells ($6000 - 8000/\text{mm}^3$):
     * *Granulocytes:* **Neutrophils** ($60–65\%$, active phagocytes with multi-lobed nucleus), **Eosinophils** ($2–3\%$, bilobed, fight parasitic helminth infections and allergic reactions), **Basophils** ($0.5–1\%$, secrete **Histamine** [vasodilator], **Serotonin** [vasoconstrictor], and **Heparin** [anticoagulant]).
     * *Agranulocytes:* **Lymphocytes** ($20–25\%$, B-cells [humoral immunity] and T-cells [cell-mediated immunity]), **Monocytes** ($6–8\%$, largest WBCs; differentiate into tissue macrophages).
  3. **Thrombocytes (Platelets):** Non-nucleated cytoplasmic fragments pinched off from giant bone marrow **Megakaryocytes** ($150,000 - 400,000/\text{mm}^3$); lifespan $7–10\text{ days}$.

```
                 BLOOD CLOTTING ENZYME CASCADE (Best & Taylor)
                 
  Injured Tissues / Damaged Platelets
                 │
                 ▼  Release THROMBOPLASTIN (Factor III / Tissue Factor)
  [Prothrombinase / Thrombokinase Complex]
                 │
                 ▼  Catalyzes in presence of CALCIUM IONS (Ca2+)
  Inactive PROTHROMBIN (Factor II) ──► Active THROMBIN (Factor IIa)
                                             │
                                             ▼
  Soluble FIBRINOGEN (Factor I) ──────► Insoluble FIBRIN MONOMERS
                                             │
                                             ▼  Polymerization (Factor XIII)
                                        FIBRIN MESH + Entrapped Blood Cells
                                             │
                                             ▼
                                        STABLE HEMOSTATIC CLOT
```

### 24.3.2 Blood Group Genetics & Rh Incompatibility
* **ABO Blood Groups (Karl Landsteiner, Nobel 1930):** Determined by genetically dictated oligosaccharide antigens on erythrocyte membranes, governed by multiple alleles ($I^A, I^B, i$):
  * **Group A:** Antigen A on RBCs; Anti-B isoantibodies in plasma (Genotypes: $I^A I^A$ or $I^A i$).
  * **Group B:** Antigen B on RBCs; Anti-A isoantibodies in plasma (Genotypes: $I^B I^B$ or $I^B i$).
  * **Group AB:** Both Antigens A and B; **NO antibodies** in plasma ($I^A I^B$). **Universal Recipient**.
  * **Group O:** **NO antigens** on RBCs; both Anti-A and Anti-B antibodies in plasma ($ii$). **Universal Donor**.
* **The Rh Factor & Erythroblastosis Fetalis:**
  * Discovered by Landsteiner and Wiener (1940) in Rhesus macaque monkeys. An individual possessing the D-antigen on RBCs is **$\text{Rh}^+$** ($\sim 85\%$ of humans); lacking it is **$\text{Rh}^-$**.
  * **Erythroblastosis Fetalis (Hemolytic Disease of the Newborn, HDN):** Occurs strictly when an **$\text{Rh}^-$ mother carries an $\text{Rh}^+$ fetus** (inherited from an $\text{Rh}^+$ father):
    1. During first delivery, fetomaternal micro-hemorrhage exposes the $\text{Rh}^-$ mother to fetal $\text{Rh}^+$ antigens, sensitizing her immune system to produce maternal **anti-Rh IgG antibodies**.
    2. In subsequent $\text{Rh}^+$ pregnancies, maternal anti-Rh IgG antibodies cross the placenta into fetal circulation, destroying fetal erythrocytes, resulting in severe hemolytic anemia, fatal kernicterus (brain damage from bilirubin), or intrauterine death.
    3. *Prophylaxis:* Administering **Anti-D immunoglobulin (RhoGAM)** to the $\text{Rh}^-$ mother within $72\text{ hours}$ of delivering every $\text{Rh}^+$ neonate, destroying escaped fetal RBCs before maternal B-cell sensitization occurs.

### 24.3.3 Cardiac Electrophysiology & The Cardiac Cycle
* **Nodal Conduction System:**
  * **Sinoatrial (SA) Node:** Located in the superior wall of right atrium near superior vena cava entry. Generates intrinsic rhythmic action potentials at the highest frequency ($\mathbf{70 - 75\text{ beats/min}}$) due to spontaneous diastolic $Na^+$ leakage (funny currents). Acts as the primary **Pacemaker of the Heart**.
  * **Atrioventricular (AV) Node:** Located in interatrial septum; introduces a crucial physiological delay ($\approx 0.1\text{ second}$), ensuring complete atrial emptying before ventricular contraction begins. Acts as the **Pacesetter**.
  * **Bundle of His & Purkinje Fibers:** Rapidly propagate action potentials through the interventricular septum to ventricular myocardium, ensuring synchronized apex-to-base ventricular ejection.
* **The Cardiac Cycle ($0.8\text{ Second}$ at $75\text{ bpm}$):**
  1. *Joint Diastole ($0.4\text{ s}$):* All four chambers relaxed; tricuspid and bicuspid (mitral) valves open; blood flows passively from atria into ventricles ($70\%$ passive ventricular filling).
  2. *Atrial Systole ($0.1\text{ s}$):* SA node fires; atria contract simultaneously, pumping remaining $30\%$ of blood into ventricles.
  3. *Ventricular Systole ($0.3\text{ s}$):* Ventricles contract; intraventricular pressure spikes:
     * **First Heart Sound ("LUB"):** Sudden closure of the **Atrioventricular (Tricuspid and Mitral) Valves** at the onset of ventricular systole. Low-pitch, long-duration ($0.14\text{ s}$).
     * Isovolumetric contraction transitions to rapid ejection into aorta and pulmonary artery.
  4. *Ventricular Diastole:* Ventricular pressure plummets:
     * **Second Heart Sound ("DUB"):** Sudden closure of the **Semilunar (Aortic and Pulmonary) Valves** at the onset of ventricular diastole. Sharp, high-pitch, short-duration ($0.08\text{ s}$).
* **Hemodynamic Formulas:**
  * **Stroke Volume (SV):** $\text{End-Diastolic Volume (EDV: } 120\text{ mL)} - \text{End-Systolic Volume (ESV: } 50\text{ mL)} = \mathbf{70\text{ mL}}$.
  * **Cardiac Output (CO):** Volume of blood pumped by each ventricle per minute:
    $$\mathbf{\text{Cardiac Output}} = \text{Stroke Volume (SV)} \times \text{Heart Rate (HR)} = 70\text{ mL} \times 72\text{ bpm} \approx \mathbf{5040\text{ mL/min}} \approx \mathbf{5\text{ Liters/min}}$$

```
                          ELECTROCARDIOGRAM (ECG) TRACING
                          
               R
               /\
              /  \
        P    /    \        T
       __   /      \      __
      /  \ /        \    /  \
     /    V          \  /    \
    ─      Q          ─S      ──────────► Time
```

* **ECG Deflection Waves:**
  * **P Wave:** Represents **Atrial Depolarization** (electrical excitation spreading from SA node across atria).
  * **QRS Complex:** Represents **Ventricular Depolarization** (initiates ventricular systole). Pathological widening ($>0.12\text{ s}$) indicates bundle branch block.
  * **T Wave:** Represents **Ventricular Repolarization** (relaxation of ventricles). Flattening or inversion signals myocardial ischemia.
  * *Counting Heart Rate:* The number of QRS complexes occurring per minute yields the heart rate.

---1a:Tc84,### Question 1 (UPSC CSE Prelims Standard)
Consider the following statements regarding human respiration and circulation:
1. Carbon monoxide poisoning results from the preferential formation of carboxyhemoglobin, which binds oxygen with irreversible stability.
2. The functional residual capacity (FRC) of lungs can be directly measured using standard clinical spirometry.
3. During the cardiac cycle, the first heart sound ("lub") is produced by the simultaneous closure of the semilunar valves.
4. Erythroblastosis fetalis occurs when an $\text{Rh}^-$ mother carries an $\text{Rh}^+$ fetus during a subsequent pregnancy.

Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
A) 4 only  
B) 1 and 4 only  
C) 2 and 3 only  
D) 1, 2 and 4 only  

**Analytical Answer with Epistemic Breakdown:**
* Statement 1 is **INCORRECT**: Carbon monoxide binds hemoglobin's iron to form carboxyhemoglobin, but it does NOT bind oxygen with irreversible stability; it physically competes with and blocks oxygen from binding hemoglobin due to its $>200\times$ higher binding affinity.
* Statement 2 is **INCORRECT**: Residual Volume (RV) cannot be exhaled and therefore **cannot be measured by standard spirometry**. Because $\text{FRC} = \text{ERV} + \text{RV}$, FRC also cannot be measured by simple spirometry.
* Statement 3 is **INCORRECT**: The first heart sound ("lub") is produced by the closure of the **Atrioventricular (Tricuspid and Mitral) valves**, not semilunar valves (which produce the second sound "dub").
* Statement 4 is **CORRECT**: An $\text{Rh}^-$ mother previously sensitized to $\text{Rh}^+$ fetal blood mounts anti-Rh IgG antibodies that cross the placenta to attack fetal RBCs in subsequent $\text{Rh}^+$ gestations.
* **Correct Option:** **A) 4 only**.

### Question 2 (Competitive State PSC Standard)
Which of the following enzymes present in human gastrointestinal secretions does NOT directly break down dietary nutrients, but is strictly required to activate pancreatic zymogens?
A) Pepsin  
B) Enterokinase (Enteropeptidase)  
C) Pancreatic Amylase  
D) Ptyalin  

**Analytical Answer:**
* **Enterokinase** (enteropeptidase) is a brush-border enzyme secreted by the duodenal mucosa whose solitary, indispensable function is to cleave inactive pancreatic **Trypsinogen into active Trypsin**, which subsequently activates chymotrypsinogen, procarboxypeptidase, and proelastase.
* **Correct Option:** **B) Enterokinase (Enteropeptidase)**.

### Question 3 (Advanced Concept Standard)
If a healthy individual has a resting heart rate of $75\text{ beats/min}$, an end-diastolic volume of $130\text{ mL}$, and an end-systolic volume of $50\text{ mL}$, what is their Cardiac Output?
A) $3.75\text{ L/min}$  
B) $5.00\text{ L/min}$  
C) $6.00\text{ L/min}$  
D) $9.75\text{ L/min}$  

**Analytical Answer:**
* Stroke Volume ($\text{SV}$) = $\text{End-Diastolic Volume (EDV)} - \text{End-Systolic Volume (ESV)}$
  $$\text{SV} = 130\text{ mL} - 50\text{ mL} = 80\text{ mL}$$
* Cardiac Output ($\text{CO}$) = $\text{Stroke Volume} \times \text{Heart Rate}$
  $$\text{CO} = 80\text{ mL} \times 75\text{ bpm} = 6000\text{ mL/min} = \mathbf{6.00\text{ L/min}}$$
* **Correct Option:** **C) 6.00 L/min**.1b:T1b62,### 23.1.1 Thermodynamic Concept of Water Potential ($\Psi_w$)
Water moves spontaneously from a region of higher chemical potential (free energy) to one of lower chemical potential. By international convention, the water potential of pure water at standard temperature and atmospheric pressure is defined as **Zero ($\Psi_w = 0\text{ MPa}$)**. Addition of solutes lowers free energy, rendering water potential negative ($\Psi_w < 0$).

$$\Psi_w = \Psi_s + \Psi_p$$

* **Solute Potential ($\Psi_s$, Osmotic Potential):** Magnitude of reduction in water potential due to the dissolution of solute molecules. Always **negative**; becomes increasingly negative as solute concentration rises.
* **Pressure Potential ($\Psi_p$, Turgor Pressure):** Hydrostatic pressure exerted by the cell sap against the rigid cellulosic cell wall. Usually **positive** in turgid cells; zero in flaccid cells; negative during extreme tension in xylem vessels.

```
       PLASMOLYSIS PROGRESSION IN PLANT CELLS
       
 [Hypertonic External Medium]    [Isotonic Medium]          [Hypotonic Medium]
       (Exosmosis)                  (Dynamic Equilibrium)      (Endosmosis)
            │                               │                        │
            ▼                               ▼                        ▼
 [Plasmolyzed Cell]              [Flaccid Cell]             [Turgid Cell]
 Protoplast shrinks completely   Protoplast in contact      Protoplast exerts high
 away from cell wall; space      with wall without turgor   TURGOR PRESSURE (Turgid;
 filled with external solution   (Turgor Pressure = 0)      highest Psi_w)
```

* **Plasmolysis:** When a plant cell is placed in a hypertonic solution, water diffuses out through the selectively permeable tonoplast and plasma membrane (**exosmosis**). The protoplast contracts away from the cell wall. The space between the shrunken protoplast and the cell wall becomes occupied by the external hypertonic solution. Plasmolysis is reversed by placing the cell in a hypotonic medium (**deplasmolysis**).
* **Imbibition:** A specialized physical adsorption of water by hydrophilic, solid colloidal matrices without forming a true solution. Driven by steep water potential gradients (e.g., swelling of dry seeds upon germination, swelling of wooden doors during humid monsoons; exerts massive imbibition pressure up to $100\text{ MPa}$).

### 23.1.2 Water Absorption Pathways in Roots
Water absorbed by root hairs crosses the cortical layers to enter the central vascular cylinder via two distinct anatomical pathways:
1. **Apoplast Pathway:** Passive movement occurring exclusively through non-living interconnected cell walls and intercellular spaces. Water moves by mass flow without crossing any selective semi-permeable membranes; offers low hydraulic resistance.
   * *The Endodermal Barrier:* The apoplastic flux is completely arrested at the **Endodermis** due to radial and transverse wall bandings impregnated with hydrophobic, waxy **Suberin**, termed the **Casparian Strips**.
2. **Symplast Pathway:** Movement through living protoplasts interconnected by cytoplasmic bridges called **Plasmodesmata**. At the endodermis, all water is forced out of the apoplast and into the symplast across the endodermal plasma membrane, establishing strict metabolic control over solute uptake.

```
   [Soil Solution] ──► [Root Hair] ──► [Cortex] ──► [Endodermis (Casparian Strip)] ──► [Pericycle] ──► [Xylem Vessels]
                                                               │
                                         (Forced into SYMPLAST by Suberin Band)
```

### 23.1.3 The Mechanism of Ascent of Sap
The vertical transport of water and dissolved inorganic ions from root tips to the uppermost canopy of tall trees (e.g., *Sequoia sempervirens*, $>110\text{ meters}$) operates via two distinct mechanisms:
1. **Root Pressure Theory (Joseph Priestley):** Active accumulation of mineral ions in root xylem sap creates an osmotic influx of water, generating a positive hydrostatic pressure (**Root Pressure**, $0.1 - 0.2\text{ MPa}$).
   * *Guttation:* Under conditions of high soil moisture and low atmospheric transpiration (e.g., humid nights/early mornings), root pressure forces droplets of liquid xylem sap through specialized marginal vein-end pores called **Hydathodes** (e.g., in grasses, strawberry, tomato). Guttation fluid contains dissolved minerals, distinguishing it from pure condensed atmospheric dew.
   * *Limitation:* Root pressure is insufficient to lift water beyond a few meters and is completely absent in tall conifers.
2. **Cohesion-Tension-Transpiration Pull Theory (Dixon & Joly, 1894):** The universally accepted physical mechanism for long-distance xylem transport.
   * Driven by **Transpiration Pull**: Evaporation of water from mesophyll cell walls into intercellular substomatal cavities generates a profound negative hydrostatic pressure (suction tension up to $-2.0\text{ to }-3.0\text{ MPa}$) transmitted down the continuous water column in xylem.
   * Maintained by intrinsic properties of water molecules:
     * **Cohesion:** High tensile strength ($>100\text{ MPa}$) due to mutual hydrogen bonding between adjacent water molecules, preventing cavitation (column breakage).
     * **Adhesion:** Electrostatic attraction between polar water molecules and the hydrophilic lignocellulosic walls of xylem tracheary elements.
     * **Surface Tension:** High surface tension at the air-water interface of mesophyll micro-capillaries.

### 23.1.4 Stomatal Mechanics: The Active Potassium ($K^+$) Pump Theory (Levitt, 1974)
Stomata are micro-pores flanked by two specialized epidermal **Guard Cells** (dumbbell-shaped in monocot grasses; kidney/bean-shaped in dicots) whose inner walls facing the pore are thick and inelastic, while outer walls are thin and elastic.
* **Daytime Opening Mechanism:**
  1. In the presence of sunlight, guard cells carry out photosynthesis or consume stored starch, synthesizing **Phosphoenolpyruvate (PEP)**, which combines with $\text{CO}_2$ via PEP Carboxylase to form **Malic Acid**.
  2. Malic acid dissociates into **Malate anions** and **$\text{H}^+$ protons**.
  3. An ATP-driven proton pump on the guard cell membrane actively expels $\text{H}^+$ into adjacent subsidiary cells while importing **Potassium ions ($K^+$)** into the guard cell, balanced by inward **Chloride ($\text{Cl}^-$)** influx.
  4. Hyper-accumulation of $K^+$ and malate inside the central vacuole dramatically lowers guard cell osmotic/water potential ($\Psi_w \ll 0$), inducing **endosmosis** of water from subsidiary cells.
  5. Increased turgor pressure causes the thin outer walls to bulge outward, pulling the thick inner walls apart, **opening the stomatal aperture**.
* **Night Closure Mechanism:** Cessation of light leads to abscisic acid (ABA) release or reversal of the proton pump, driving $K^+$ and malate efflux, causing guard cell flaccidity and stomatal closure.

---1c:T14b0,### 23.2.1 Essential Mineral Elements (Arnon & Stout Criteria, 1939)
An element is declared strictly essential if:
1. The plant cannot complete its vegetative or reproductive life cycle in its total absence.
2. The requirement is specific and non-substitutable by any other element.
3. The element is directly involved in plant nutrition and cellular metabolism (structural or enzymatic).

* **Macronutrients (Concentration $> 10\text{ mmol/kg}$ dry matter):** Carbon, Hydrogen, Oxygen, Nitrogen, Phosphorus, Potassium, Calcium, Magnesium, Sulfur ($\text{C, H, O, N, P, K, Ca, Mg, S}$).
* **Micronutrients / Trace Elements ($< 10\text{ mmol/kg}$ dry matter):** Iron, Manganese, Copper, Molybdenum, Zinc, Boron, Chlorine, Nickel ($\text{Fe, Mn, Cu, Mo, Zn, B, Cl, Ni}$).

| Element | Metabolic Role & Enzymatic Activations | Diagnostic Deficiency Symptoms |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Nitrogen ($\text{N}$)** | Constituent of amino acids, proteins, nucleic acids, chlorophyll, and vitamins; absorbed as $\text{NO}_3^-$ or $\text{NH}_4^+$. | Generalized **Chlorosis** (yellowing) beginning first in older mature leaves; stunted growth. |
| **Phosphorus ($\text{P}$)** | Constituent of ATP, phospholipids, nucleotides; key in phosphorylation reactions; absorbed as $\text{H}_2\text{PO}_4^-$. | Dark green or purple/bronze foliar pigmentation (anthocyanin accumulation); poor root development. |
| **Potassium ($\text{K}$)** | Osmoregulation; stomatal opening/closing ($K^+$ pump); maintains anion-cation cellular balance. | Marginal chlorosis and **necrosis of leaf tips and margins** ("marginal scorch"). |
| **Calcium ($\text{Ca}$)** | Forms **Calcium Pectate** in middle lamella of cell walls; stabilizes mitotic spindle fibers and membranes. | Stunted root tips; necrotic growing apical buds (**immobile element**: symptoms first in young tissues). |
| **Magnesium ($\text{Mg}$)** | **Central coordinating atom of the Chlorophyll porphyrin ring**; essential activator of RuBisCO and PEP carboxylase. | Severe **Interveinal Chlorosis** appearing first in older leaves (highly mobile element). |
| **Sulfur ($\text{S}$)** | Present in amino acids **Cysteine and Methionine**, coenzyme A, thiamine, biotin, and ferredoxin. | Chlorosis appearing primarily in young leaves first (relatively immobile). |
| **Iron ($\text{Fe}$)** | Component of ferredoxin and cytochromes; essential activator for **catalase** and chlorophyll biosynthesis. | Severe interveinal chlorosis of younger leaves. |
| **Zinc ($\text{Zn}$)** | Required for synthesis of **Auxin (IAA)**; activates carbonic anhydrase and alcohol dehydrogenase. | **"Little Leaf" disease**; rosette leaves; chlorosis. |
| **Manganese ($\text{Mn}$)** | Essential cofactor for **Photolysis of Water** in Photosystem II ($2\text{H}_2\text{O} \to \text{O}_2 + 4\text{H}^+ + 4e^-$). | **"Marsh Spot" disease** of peas; grey specks in oats. |
| **Molybdenum ($\text{Mo}$)** | Critical structural component of **Nitrogenase** and **Nitrate Reductase** complexes. | **"Whiptail" disease** of cauliflower; defective leaf blades. |
| **Boron ($\text{B}$)** | Required for uptake and utilization of $\text{Ca}^{2+}$, **Pollen grain germination**, and carbohydrate phloem translocation. | Death of root and shoot tips; "heart rot" of sugar beet. |

### 23.2.2 Biological Nitrogen Fixation (BNF)
Molecular atmospheric dinitrogen ($\text{N}\equiv\text{N}$) possesses an extraordinarily stable covalent triple bond requiring immense energy ($945\text{ kJ/mol}$) to break. Only prokaryotes possessing the enzyme complex **Nitrogenase** can reduce atmospheric nitrogen to ammonia ($\text{NH}_3$).

```
                    SYMBIOTIC NITROGEN FIXATION IN ROOT NODULES
                    
  [Rhizobium Leguminosarum] ──► Attracted by plant flavonoids / lectins to root hair
             │
             ▼ Root hair curls; Rhizobium enters via INFECTION THREAD
  [Cortical Cell Division]  ──► Forms Root Nodule housing modified bacteroids
             │
             ▼ High [O2] is LETHAL to Nitrogenase enzyme!
  [LEGHEMOGLOBIN Synthesis] ──► Pink/red oxygen-scavenging hemeprotein maintains
                                 anoxic micro-environment for active Nitrogenase
```

* **The Overall Stoichiometry of Biological Nitrogen Fixation:**
  $$\text{N}_2 + 8\text{e}^- + 8\text{H}^+ + 16\text{ATP} \xrightarrow{\text{Nitrogenase}} 2\text{NH}_3 + \text{H}_2 + 16\text{ADP} + 16\text{P}_i$$
  * *Bioenergetic Cost:* Fixing a single molecule of $\text{N}_2$ into two molecules of $\text{NH}_3$ consumes **$16\text{ ATP}$ molecules** ($8\text{ ATP}$ per ammonia generated), derived from host plant respiration.
  * *Composition of Nitrogenase:* A two-component metalloenzyme consisting of a smaller **Fe-protein (Dinitrogenase reductase)** and a larger **Mo-Fe protein (Dinitrogenase)**.
  * *The Oxygen Dilemma & Leghemoglobin:* Nitrogenase is irreversibly inactivated by molecular oxygen. Legume nodules solve this through the expression of **Leghemoglobin**—a symbiotic pink/red chromoprotein (globin synthesized by plant, heme by bacterium) acting as a high-affinity **Oxygen Scavenger**, delivering oxygen to bacteroid respiratory chains for ATP synthesis while maintaining an ultralow free oxygen tension in the catalytic zone.

---1d:T11c7,Photosynthesis is an anabolic, endergonic oxidation-reduction process in which light energy converts water and atmospheric carbon dioxide into carbohydrates, evolving oxygen:

$$6\text{CO}_2 + 12\text{H}_2\text{O} \xrightarrow[\text{Chlorophyll}]{\text{Light Energy}} \text{C}_6\text{H}_{12}\text{O}_6 + 6\text{H}_2\text{O} + 6\text{O}_2$$

(Demonstrated by **C.B. van Niel** using purple sulfur bacteria that $\text{O}_2$ evolves strictly from the photolysis of $\text{H}_2\text{O}$, not from $\text{CO}_2$; verified isotopically by Ruben, Kamen et al. using $^{18}\text{O}$).

```
                     LIGHT-DEPENDENT REACTIONS (Z-SCHEME)
                     
      Energy
        ▲
        │                    [Primary Acceptor]                [Primary Acceptor]
        │                           │                                  │
        │                           ▼ (Plastoquinone,                  ▼ (Ferredoxin)
        │                             Cyt b6f, PC)                     │
        │                     ┌─────►                                  ▼
        │                     │                                  [NADP+ Reductase]
        │             [PS II (P680)]                           NADP+ + H+ ──► NADPH
        │                     ▲
        │                     │
        │             [Photolysis of H2O]
        │             2H2O ──► O2 + 4H+ + 4e-
        │
        └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────►
```

### 23.3.1 Photosystems & Pigment Architecture
Pigments are organized into discrete **Light Harvesting Complexes (LHC)** or **Photosystems** embedded within the thylakoid membrane:
1. **Photosystem II (PS II):** Reaction center chlorophyll *a* absorbs optimally at **$680\text{ nm}$ ($P_{680}$)**. Located predominantly in the appressed (stacked) thylakoid membranes of grana.
2. **Photosystem I (PS I):** Reaction center chlorophyll *a* absorbs optimally at **$700\text{ nm}$ ($P_{700}$)**. Located in non-appressed stroma lamellae and outer granal margins.

### 23.3.2 Non-Cyclic vs. Cyclic Photophosphorylation

| Parameter | Non-Cyclic Photophosphorylation (Z-Scheme) | Cyclic Photophosphorylation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Photosystems Involved** | Both **PS II and PS I** operate sequentially. | Operates strictly via **PS I only**. |
| **Electron Source & Flow** | Continuous supply from **Photolysis of $\text{H}_2\text{O}$**; unidirectional, open electron flow ($\text{H}_2\text{O} \to \text{PS II} \to \text{Cyt } b_6f \to \text{PS I} \to \text{NADP}^+$). | No photolysis; electrons expelled from $P_{700}$ return cyclically back to $P_{700}$ via cytochrome cascade. |
| **Photolysis of Water** | **Occurs** at Oxygen Evolving Complex (OEC) mediated by $\text{Mn}^{2+}, \text{Ca}^{2+}, \text{Cl}^-$. | **Absent**; no water consumed. |
| **Oxygen Evolution** | **Yes ($\text{O}_2$ is evolved)** into atmosphere. | **No oxygen evolved**. |
| **End Products** | Synthesizes **ATP, NADPH, and $\text{O}_2$**. | Synthesizes **ATP only** (No NADPH produced). |
| **Dominant Location** | Grana thylakoid membranes. | Stroma lamellae (which lack PS II and NADP reductase). |

### 23.3.3 The Chemiosmotic Hypothesis (Peter Mitchell, 1961)
ATP synthesis is driven by an electrochemical **Proton Gradient ($\Delta \mu_{\text{H}^+}$)** established across the thylakoid membrane, characterized by high proton concentration (acidic $\text{pH} \approx 4.5$) inside the **Thylakoid Lumen** and low proton concentration (alkaline $\text{pH} \approx 8.0$) in the **Stroma**.
* **Protons Accumulate in the Lumen Due To:**
  1. Photolysis of water on the inner luminal face releases $\text{H}^+$ into the lumen.
  2. Plastoquinone ($PQ$), a mobile hydrogen carrier, picks up protons from the stroma while transporting electrons, pumping them across the membrane into the lumen.
  3. Protons in the stroma are consumed by NADP Reductase to reduce $\text{NADP}^+$ into $\text{NADPH} + \text{H}^+$.
* **ATP Synthesis:** Protons diffuse down their steep electrochemical gradient back into the stroma exclusively through the transmembrane channel of the **$\text{CF}_0$** subunit of ATP Synthase. The conformational rotation of the catalytic **$\text{CF}_1$** headpiece drives the phosphorylation of $\text{ADP} + \text{P}_i \longrightarrow \text{ATP}$.

---1e:T1331,Dark reactions (light-independent reactions) take place in the stroma of chloroplasts, utilizing ATP and NADPH generated in light reactions to reduce $\text{CO}_2$ into hexose sugars.

```
                           CARBON FIXATION DIVERGENCE
                                        │
           ┌────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┐
           ▼                            ▼                            ▼
     [C3 PATHWAY]                 [C4 PATHWAY]                 [CAM PATHWAY]
   - Calvin Cycle               - Hatch-Slack Pathway        - Crassulacean Acid Metabolism
   - 1st Product: 3-PGA         - 1st Product: OAA (4C)      - Temporal Separation (Night/Day)
   - RuBisCO fixes CO2          - Kranz Anatomy              - Scotoactive Stomata
   - High Photorespiration      - Zero Photorespiration      - Desert Succulents
```

### 23.4.1 The Calvin Cycle ($C_3$ Pathway) (Melvin Calvin, Nobel 1961)
Operates in all photosynthetic plants (wheat, rice, barley, soybeans). Comprises three sequential enzymatic stages:
1. **Carboxylation:** Atmospheric $\text{CO}_2$ combines with the 5-carbon primary acceptor molecule **Ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate (RuBP)**, catalyzed by **RuBisCO (RuBP Carboxylase-Oxygenase)**, forming two molecules of the 3-carbon acid **3-Phosphoglyceric Acid (3-PGA)**—the first stable intermediate.
2. **Reduction:** 3-PGA is phosphorylated and reduced into Triose Phosphate (Glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate, G3P). To produce one molecule of glucose, $6\text{ cycles}$ are required.
3. **Regeneration of RuBP:** Requires ATP phosphorylation to maintain cyclic operation.
* **Energetic Balance Sheet per Glucose Molecule ($\text{C}_6\text{H}_{12}\text{O}_6$):**
  $$\text{Fixation of } 6\text{ CO}_2 \implies \mathbf{18\text{ ATP}} + \mathbf{12\text{ NADPH}} \text{ consumed}.$$
  *(Per single $\text{CO}_2$ molecule: $3\text{ ATP} + 2\text{ NADPH}$).*

### 23.4.2 Photorespiration ($C_2$ Cycle / Photosynthetic Carbon Oxidation)
RuBisCO is a bifunctional enzyme with active sites binding both $\text{CO}_2$ and $\text{O}_2$ competitively. Under high light intensity, elevated temperatures ($>25^\circ\text{C}$), and high $\text{O}_2/\text{CO}_2$ ratios, RuBisCO functions as an **Oxygenase**:

$$\text{RuBP (5C)} + \text{O}_2 \xrightarrow{\text{RuBisCO}} \text{3-PGA (3C)} + \text{2-Phosphoglycolate (2C)}$$

* **Compartmental Coordination:** Operates across three coordinated organelles: **Chloroplast $\longrightarrow$ Peroxisome $\longrightarrow$ Mitochondria**.
* **Thermodynamic Wastefulness:** Photorespiration consumes ATP and oxygen, releases previously fixed $\text{CO}_2$ in mitochondria without producing ATP, NADPH, or sugars, squandering up to $25–40\%$ of photosynthetic energy in $C_3$ crops.

### 23.4.3 The $C_4$ Pathway (Hatch-Slack Pathway)
Evolved in tropical plants (Maize, Sugarcane, Sorghum, *Amaranthus*) to completely bypass photorespiration through spatial compartmentalization:
* **Kranz Anatomy:** Leaves display two concentric rings of photosynthetic tissue around vascular bundles:
  1. *Mesophyll Cells:* Lack RuBisCO; contain high levels of **PEP Carboxylase (PEPCase)**, which has high affinity for $\text{HCO}_3^-$ and zero affinity for oxygen. Primary $\text{CO}_2$ acceptor is **Phosphoenolpyruvate (PEP, 3C)**, forming the first stable product, **Oxaloacetate (OAA, 4C)**. OAA is reduced to Malate and transported to bundle sheath cells via plasmodesmata.
  2. *Bundle Sheath Cells:* Large, thick-walled, suberized cells lacking intercellular spaces, containing agranal chloroplasts rich in **RuBisCO**. Malate undergoes decarboxylation:
     $$\text{Malate (4C)} \longrightarrow \text{Pyruvate (3C)} + \text{CO}_2$$
     The released $\text{CO}_2$ elevates bundle-sheath $[\text{CO}_2]$ to extraordinarily high levels, saturating RuBisCO's carboxylase activity and **completely suppressing photorespiration**. Pyruvate returns to mesophyll cells to regenerate PEP.
* **Energetic Cost:** Fixing $1\text{ CO}_2$ requires $5\text{ ATP} + 2\text{ NADPH}$ ($30\text{ ATP}$ per glucose molecule; higher than $C_3$'s $18\text{ ATP}$, but dramatically more efficient under high tropical temperatures and light).

### 23.4.4 CAM Pathway (Crassulacean Acid Metabolism)
Adapted for extreme arid xerophytic environments (cacti, pineapple, *Agave*, *Vanilla*):
* **Temporal Separation:**
  * *Night (Scotoactive Stomata Open):* To conserve water, stomata open strictly at night. $\text{CO}_2$ is fixed by PEPCase into OAA, reduced to Malic Acid, and stored in large central vacuoles.
  * *Day (Stomata Tightly Closed):* Malic acid is released from vacuoles, decarboxylated to yield $\text{CO}_2$, which enters the Calvin cycle driven by light reactions, while transpiration water loss is reduced by $90\%$.

---1f:T163b,Cellular respiration is an oxidative, exergonic catabolic process degrading organic hexose fuels to produce ATP.

```
                      CELLULAR RESPIRATION FLOWCHART
                      
           [Glucose (6C)]
                 │
                 ▼  GLYCOLYSIS (Cytoplasm / Anaerobic, Net: 2 ATP + 2 NADH)
           [2 Pyruvate (3C)]
                 │
                 ▼  LINK REACTION (Mitochondrial Matrix, 2 NADH + 2 CO2)
           [2 Acetyl-CoA (2C)]
                 │
                 ▼  KREBS CYCLE (TCA Cycle, Matrix, 2 GTP + 6 NADH + 2 FADH2 + 4 CO2)
           [Reducing Equivalents (NADH, FADH2)]
                 │
                 ▼  ELECTRON TRANSPORT CHAIN (Inner Mitochondrial Membrane)
           [ATP Synthesis via Oxidative Phosphorylation]
```

### 23.5.1 Glycolysis (EMP Pathway — Embden, Meyerhof, Parnas)
Occurs in the **Cytoplasm** of all living cells; anaerobic (requires zero $\text{O}_2$); universal metabolic pathway:
* Converts 1 molecule of Glucose ($6\text{C}$) into 2 molecules of **Pyruvate ($3\text{C}$)** via 10 enzyme-catalyzed steps:
  * *Preparatory Phase (Energy Investment):* Phosphorylation consumes **$2\text{ ATP}$**. Key regulatory pacemaker step is catalyzed by **Phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1)**:
    $$\text{Fructose-6-Phosphate} + \text{ATP} \xrightarrow{\text{PFK-1}} \text{Fructose-1,6-bisphosphate} + \text{ADP}$$
  * *Pay-Off Phase (Energy Yield):* Generates **$4\text{ ATP}$** (via substrate-level phosphorylation) and **$2\text{ NADH} + 2\text{H}^+$**.
* **Net Glycolytic Yield:** $\mathbf{2\text{ ATP}} + \mathbf{2\text{ NADH}} + \mathbf{2\text{ Pyruvate}}$.

### 23.5.2 Fermentation (Anaerobic Fate of Pyruvate)
In the absence of oxygen, pyruvate cannot enter mitochondria; NADH must be reoxidized to $\text{NAD}^+$ to permit continued glycolysis:
1. **Alcoholic Fermentation (Yeast):** Pyruvate is decarboxylated by *Pyruvate Decarboxylase* to Acetaldehyde, which is reduced by *Alcohol Dehydrogenase* to **Ethanol**:
   $$\text{Pyruvate} \longrightarrow \text{Acetaldehyde} + \text{CO}_2 \xrightarrow{\text{NADH} \to \text{NAD}^+} \text{Ethanol } (\text{C}_2\text{H}_5\text{OH})$$
   (Yeast cells poison themselves when alcohol concentration reaches $\sim 13\%$).
2. **Lactic Acid Fermentation (Lactic Acid Bacteria & Fatigued Human Skeletal Muscle):**
   $$\text{Pyruvate} + \text{NADH} + \text{H}^+ \xrightarrow{\text{Lactate Dehydrogenase}} \text{Lactate} + \text{NAD}^+$$
   Accumulation of lactic acid in myocytes induces muscular fatigue and cramping.

### 23.5.3 The Link Reaction (Oxidative Decarboxylation of Pyruvate)
Pyruvate enters the mitochondrial matrix via a pyruvate translocase:

$$\text{2 Pyruvate (3C)} + 2\text{CoA-SH} + 2\text{NAD}^+ \xrightarrow{\text{Pyruvate Dehydrogenase Complex}} 2\text{Acetyl-CoA (2C)} + 2\text{CO}_2 + 2\text{NADH} + 2\text{H}^+$$

### 23.5.4 The Krebs Cycle (Tricarboxylic Acid / Citric Acid Cycle) (Hans Krebs, 1937)
Occurs in the **Mitochondrial Matrix**:
* Acetyl-CoA ($2\text{C}$) condenses with Oxaloacetate (OAA, $4\text{C}$) via *Citrate Synthase* to form **Citrate ($6\text{C}$)**.
* Sequence: Citrate $\to$ Isocitrate $\to$ $\alpha$-Ketoglutarate ($5\text{C}$) $\to$ Succinyl-CoA ($4\text{C}$) $\to$ Succinate $\to$ Fumarate $\to$ Malate $\to$ Oxaloacetate.
* **Per Acetyl-CoA Turn:** Generates $3\text{ NADH} + 1\text{ FADH}_2 + 1\text{ GTP (ATP via SLP)} + 2\text{ CO}_2$.
* *(Per Glucose Molecule = 2 turns: $\mathbf{6\text{ NADH}} + \mathbf{2\text{ FADH}}_2 + \mathbf{2\text{ GTP}} + \mathbf{4\text{ CO}}_2$).*
* *Marker Enzyme:* **Succinate Dehydrogenase** (converts succinate to fumarate using FAD) is the *only* TCA enzyme physically embedded in the **inner mitochondrial membrane** (as Complex II of the ETC).

### 23.5.5 Electron Transport Chain (ETC) & ATP Balance
Embedded in the inner mitochondrial membrane:
* **Complex I:** NADH Dehydrogenase (oxidizes NADH, pumps $4\text{ H}^+$).
* **Complex II:** Succinate Dehydrogenase (oxidizes $\text{FADH}_2$, pumps zero $\text{ H}^+$).
* **Complex III:** Cytochrome $bc_1$ complex (pumps $4\text{ H}^+$).
* **Complex IV:** Cytochrome $c$ Oxidase (contains cytochromes $a, a_3$ and copper centers; reduces terminal $\text{O}_2$ to $\text{H}_2\text{O}$; pumps $2\text{ H}^+$). Cyanide and carbon monoxide block Complex IV.
* **Complex V:** $F_0-F_1$ ATP Synthase.
* **Stoichiometric ATP Equivalents:**
  * Oxidation of $1\text{ NADH} \implies \approx \mathbf{2.5\text{ ATP}}$ (classically $3\text{ ATP}$).
  * Oxidation of $1\text{ FADH}_2 \implies \approx \mathbf{1.5\text{ ATP}}$ (classically $2\text{ ATP}$).
  * Complete aerobic oxidation of $1\text{ molecule of glucose}$ yields **$\mathbf{30\text{ to }32\text{ ATP}}$** (modern) or **$\mathbf{36\text{ to }38\text{ ATP}}$** (classical).

### 23.5.6 Respiratory Quotient (RQ)
The volumetric ratio of $\text{CO}_2$ evolved to $\text{O}_2$ consumed in respiration:

$$\text{RQ} = \frac{\text{Volume of CO}_2\text{ evolved}}{\text{Volume of O}_2\text{ consumed}}$$

1. **Carbohydrates (Glucose):** $\text{C}_6\text{H}_{12}\text{O}_6 + 6\text{O}_2 \longrightarrow 6\text{CO}_2 + 6\text{H}_2\text{O} \implies \mathbf{\text{RQ} = \frac{6}{6} = 1.0}$.
2. **Fats (Tripalmitin):** $2\text{C}_{51}\text{H}_{98}\text{O}_6 + 145\text{O}_2 \longrightarrow 102\text{CO}_2 + 98\text{H}_2\text{O} \implies \mathbf{\text{RQ} = \frac{102}{145} \approx 0.7}$.
3. **Proteins:** $\mathbf{\text{RQ} \approx 0.8 - 0.9}$.
4. **Organic Acids (Malic Acid):** Highly oxidized fuels; $\mathbf{\text{RQ} > 1.0}$ (Malic acid $\text{RQ} = 1.33$).
5. **CAM Succulents at Night:** Incomplete oxidation without $\text{CO}_2$ release $\implies \mathbf{\text{RQ} = 0}$.

---20:Tc50,Organic signaling molecules synthesized in trace quantities, regulating growth, development, and environmental responses.

```
                                 PHYTOHORMONES
                                       │
           ┌───────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┐
           ▼                                                       ▼
  [GROWTH PROMOTERS]                                      [GROWTH INHIBITORS]
  - Auxins (Cell elongation, Apical dominance)            - Abscisic Acid (Stress hormone, Stomatal closure)
  - Gibberellins (Stem elongation, Bolting, Germination)  - Ethylene (Gaseous ripening hormone, Senescence)
  - Cytokinins (Cell division, Delays senescence)
```

| Phytohormone | Discovery Milestone & Chemical Nature | Key Physiological Effects | Major Agricultural / Horticultural Applications |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Auxins** | F.W. Went (1928, Avena coleoptile curvature test); Indole-3-acetic acid (IAA) derived from **Tryptophan**. | **Apical Dominance** (terminal apical bud suppresses lateral axillary buds); cell elongation; phototropism. | Synthetic auxins (**NAA, IBA**) induce root initiation in stem cuttings; **2,4-D** used as selective broadleaf weedicide; induces seedless fruits (**parthenocarpy** in tomatoes). |
| **Gibberellins** | E. Kurosawa (1926, "Bakanae" / foolish seedling disease of rice caused by fungus *Gibberella fujikuroi*); Terpenoid ($\text{GA}_3$). | **Internodal stem elongation**; induces **Bolting** (sudden internode elongation prior to flowering in rosette plants like cabbage, beet); breaks seed dormancy. | Spraying sugarcane increases stem yield by $20\text{ tonnes/acre}$; elongates apple fruit stalks; accelerates **malting process** in brewing industry. |
| **Cytokinins** | Skoog & Miller (1955, isolated **Kinetin** from autoclaved herring sperm DNA); natural cytokinin **Zeatin** from corn kernels. Aminopurine derivative. | Promotes **Cytokinesis / cell division**; counters apical dominance (promotes lateral shoot branching); delays leaf senescence (**Richmond-Lang Effect**). | Used in commercial plant tissue culture (High Cytokinin/Auxin ratio promotes **shoot regeneration**; low ratio promotes **root development**). |
| **Abscisic Acid (ABA)** | Addicott et al. (1963, "Abscisin II" / "Dormin"); Sesquiterpenoid. | **General Growth Inhibitor**; induces **Stomatal Closure** during water deficit (**"Stress Hormone"**); induces seed dormancy; inhibits viviparous germination. | Exogenous spray antitranspirant; ensures seed storage longevity. Antagonist to Gibberellins. |
| **Ethylene** | Cousins (1910, volatile gas from ripe oranges riped bananas); simple gaseous hydrocarbon ($\text{H}_2\text{C}=\text{CH}_2$). | Accelerates **Fruit Ripening** (respiratory climacteric); promotes abscission of leaves and flowers; induces epinasty (downward curling). | Commercial fruit ripening using **Ethephon** (aqueous solution releasing ethylene); accelerates ripening in tomatoes, mangoes; induces flowering in pineapple. |

---21:T5ad,### 23.7.1 Photoperiodism (Garner & Allard, 1920)
The physiological response of plants to the relative duration of day and night (photoperiod):
* **Phytochrome Pigment:** A homodimeric chromoprotein photoreceptor existing in two interconvertible forms:
  * **$P_r$ Form:** Absorbs **Red Light ($\lambda = 660\text{ nm}$)**; biologically inactive.
  * **$P_{fr}$ Form:** Absorbs **Far-Red Light ($\lambda = 730\text{ nm}$)**; biologically active form that regulates flowering.
* **Photoperiodic Categories:**
  1. *Short-Day Plants (SDPs / Long-Night Plants):* Flower only when photoperiod is *less* than a critical photoperiod (require an uninterrupted critical dark period, e.g., Soybean, Tobacco, Rice). Flash of light during night suppresses flowering.
  2. *Long-Day Plants (LDPs / Short-Night Plants):* Flower when day length *exceeds* critical photoperiod (e.g., Wheat, Radish, Spinach, Sugar beet).
  3. *Day-Neutral Plants (DNPs):* Flowering is independent of day length (e.g., Tomato, Sunflower, Maize).

### 23.7.2 Vernalization (Lysenko)
The quantitative or qualitative requirement for exposure to low temperatures ($1–6^\circ\text{C}$) to induce flowering in biennials and winter annuals (e.g., winter varieties of wheat and rye). Prevents premature reproductive development during autumn, ensuring flowering occurs during favorable post-winter spring conditions. Mediated by the hypothetical flowering hormone precursor **Vernalin**.

---22:Tacf,### Question 1 (UPSC CSE Prelims Standard)
Consider the following statements regarding photosynthesis in higher plants:
1. In $C_4$ plants, the primary carboxylation occurs in the bundle sheath cells catalyzed by RuBisCO.
2. Photorespiration does not produce ATP or NADPH and leads to a net loss of fixed carbon.
3. Cyclic photophosphorylation involves both Photosystem I and Photosystem II, evolving molecular oxygen.
4. The oxygen released during oxygenic photosynthesis originates from carbon dioxide molecules.

Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
A) 2 only  
B) 1 and 2 only  
C) 2 and 4 only  
D) 1, 3 and 4 only  

**Analytical Answer with Epistemic Breakdown:**
* Statement 1 is **INCORRECT**: In $C_4$ plants, primary carboxylation occurs in **mesophyll cells** catalyzed by PEP Carboxylase (forming OAA). RuBisCO operates downstream in bundle sheath cells.
* Statement 2 is **CORRECT**: Photorespiration (the $C_2$ glycolate pathway) consumes ATP and oxygen, yields no NADPH or energy reserves, and releases previously fixed $\text{CO}_2$, representing an energetic waste.
* Statement 3 is **INCORRECT**: Cyclic photophosphorylation operates exclusively through **Photosystem I (PS I)**, produces ATP only, and involves no photolysis of water or $\text{O}_2$ evolution.
* Statement 4 is **INCORRECT**: Oxygen evolved originates exclusively from the **photolysis of water ($\text{H}_2\text{O}$)**, proven experimentally by van Niel and verified using $^{18}\text{O}$.
* **Correct Option:** **A) 2 only**.

### Question 2 (Competitive State PSC Standard)
Which phytohormone is widely utilized by farmers as a selective chemical herbicide to eliminate broadleaf dicotyledonous weeds in cereal monocot crops?
A) Indole-3-acetic acid (IAA)  
B) 2,4-Dichlorophenoxyacetic acid (2,4-D)  
C) Abscisic Acid (ABA)  
D) Gibberellic Acid ($\text{GA}_3$)  

**Analytical Answer:**
* **2,4-D** is a synthetic auxin that selectively kills broadleaf dicot weeds while leaving mature monocot grass crops unharmed, making it a universal selective weedicide.
* **Correct Option:** **B) 2,4-Dichlorophenoxyacetic acid (2,4-D)**.

### Question 3 (Advanced Concept Standard)
During the complete aerobic respiration of one mole of tripalmitin (fat), what is the calculated Respiratory Quotient (RQ)?
A) $1.0$  
B) $0.7$  
C) $0.9$  
D) $1.33$  

**Analytical Answer:**
* Fats are poor in oxygen relative to carbon and hydrogen atoms, requiring substantially more oxygen molecules for combustion:
  $$2\text{C}_{51}\text{H}_{98}\text{O}_6 + 145\text{O}_2 \longrightarrow 102\text{CO}_2 + 98\text{H}_2\text{O}$$
  $$\text{RQ} = \frac{\text{CO}_2 \text{ evolved}}{\text{O}_2 \text{ consumed}} = \frac{102}{145} \approx \mathbf{0.7}$$
* **Correct Option:** **B) 0.7**.23:T2836,Excretion is the physiological elimination of metabolic nitrogenous byproducts (ammonia, urea, uric acid) and excess electrolytes to maintain water-electrolyte and acid-base homeostasis.

```
                           MODES OF NITROGENOUS EXCRETION
                                         │
            ┌────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┐
            ▼                            ▼                            ▼
     [AMMONOTELISM]                [UREOTELISM]                 [URICOTELISM]
   - Ammonia (NH3)               - Urea (H2N-CO-NH2)          - Uric Acid (Purine ring)
   - Highly toxic                - Moderate toxicity          - Least toxic, insoluble
   - Requires 300 - 500 mL       - Requires ~50 mL H2O/g      - Requires < 10 mL H2O/g
     water per gram NH3            (Synthesized in LIVER        (Excreted as dry pellet / paste)
   - Aquatic invertebrates,        via Ornithine cycle)       - Reptiles, Birds, Insects,
     bony fishes, tadpoles       - Mammals, adult amphibians,   land snails (Xeric adaptation)
                                   marine sharks
